# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-01

## LLM Agents
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Hippo: Training-Free Agent Self-Evolution via Hippocampal Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24453v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivated by recursive self-improvement (RSI), we study how LLM agents can learn through external memory without updating model parameters. Extracting useful experience from a single attempt is difficult: flawed reasoning can produce success, while poor execution can obscure a sound strategy. Preserving its value over time is also difficult: our accumulation baseline without consolidation initially improves but eventually performs below a no-memory baseline. Our key idea is to compare attempts at the same task to identify reusable decisions and refine when they apply. We introduce Hippo, a memory framework inspired by selective hippocampal encoding and replay that learns from these comparisons when shortcomings are diagnosed. It compares the resulting experience with existing memory to merge overlaps and resolve conflicts through explicit applicability conditions. This allows prior guidance to be revised without storing every lesson as a separate entry. Experiments on SWE-bench Verified, WebArena, and Mind2Web show performance gains in all three settings and substantially fewer retained experiences than the accumulation baseline. The gains extend across the evaluated base models of varying capabilities, indicating that memory-based adaptation remains useful even for highly capable models.</p></details> |  |
| **[Understanding and Enhancing Backdoor Persistency in LLM Agent Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07510v3)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Developers can build LLM agents by adapting third-party models through benign post-training. We study a supply-chain threat in which an attacker supplies a model with a backdoor: hidden behavior that produces malicious outputs when a particular input pattern appears. Focusing on software-engineering agents, we ask whether such backdoors survive the developer's supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and subsequent task-level reinforcement learning (RL). We observe that benign SFT substantially reduces attack success, but subsequent RL often preserves the residual behavior and sometimes even increases attack success. Our analysis of backdoor erosion during SFT identifies two factors that may favor survival: initial backdoor strength and gradient compatibility with benign training. These factors motivate PersistBD, which refines an already-backdoored model before release to improve its persistency through the benign post-training process. On Qwen2.5-Coder-7B, PersistBD raises attack success from 20% to 74% after SFT and from 20% to 76% after SFT-RL, while maintaining comparable benign task performance. Together, our results show that backdoors can remain active through benign post-training and that adversaries can deliberately increase their persistence. This highlights a supply-chain risk for AI developers and motivates stronger techniques for detecting and mitigating inherited backdoors when adapting third-party models into agents. Our code is available at https://github.com/uiuc-kang-lab/PersistBD.</p></details> | EMNLP 2026 Findings |
| **[Agentic LLM reasoning in pediatric diabetes outcome prediction from multimodal medical records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40379v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are proposed for clinical prediction but are untested in pediatrics, where cohorts are small and outcomes are uncommon. Using records from 2,489 youth with type 2 diabetes (T2D) and 4,018 with type 1 diabetes (T1D), spanning structured data, note-derived social determinants and, in T1D, continuous glucose monitoring data, we predicted seven T2D outcomes, four also in T1D, at 24 months from 18 months of follow-up. CEDAR (Clinical Evidence-Driven Agent for Risk-prediction), an evidence-citing, self-auditing LLM pipeline, outperformed trained machine learning in both cohorts (pooled area under the receiver operating characteristic curve 0.815 versus 0.656 to 0.700 in T2D and 0.756 versus 0.603 to 0.631 in T1D), most on the sparsely screened outcomes. Only 4 of 70,246 values it cited did not match the chart. Reading only the evidence and reasoning cited by CEDAR, two pediatric endocrinologists reached the same prediction as CEDAR in about nine of ten cases, agreeing with each other on 90.5%.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages main text plus 40 pages Supplementary Information</p></details> |
| **[The Athlete as a Scientist of One: Agentic Bayesian Experimental Design for Individualized Physiology from Consumer Wearables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40370v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents for personal health read wearable data and recommend, but do not learn about the individual in a way that can be audited or falsified. We present EpiCoach, an agentic system that treats one athlete, a cross-country mountain biker, as the subject of accountable N-of-1 science. Claims about the athlete live in an evidence-gated hypothesis registry: each must issue a probabilistic prediction about a future session before it occurs, and its belief moves only through computed evidence from a logged statistical fit. Predictions come from a mechanistic digital twin inferred from heart rate, GPS and barometric altitude: a lagged heart-rate response model, a fitness-fatigue model with learned time constants, and a latent technical-descending skill state, measured on discovered trail segments. The agent prescribes the next training week as a constrained experiment maximizing information gain subject to load and safety constraints, around two instrument rides. We evaluate the system two ways over eighteen months of data. A rolling-origin replay benchmark shows the twin beats standard forecasters on heart rate but not segment time, and the agentic loop matches a static model. A live prospective phase runs the weekly cycle: in its first three weeks the ledger sealed 20 predictions, scored 14, the descent forecast calibrated after one correction, the climb forecast pessimistic as the athlete improved, and the safety officer vetoed the agent twice. Every correction is preserved in the registry, a template for auditable, falsifiable personalization in digital health. We release the registry, benchmark and protocol.</p></details> |  |
| **[Don't Lose the Thread: Empowering Long-Horizon LLM Agents with Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40350v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents powered by large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable progress in solving complex reasoning tasks. However, LLM agents often falter on long-horizon tasks due to cognitive overload, as their working memory becomes cluttered with expanding and irrelevant information, which dilutes their attention and hinders effective planning and reasoning. To mitigate this challenge, we introduce Cognitive Resource Self-Allocation (CORAL), a novel reasoning paradigm that empowers agents to proactively optimize their context. Implemented as an agent-callable working memory management toolset, CORAL allows an agent to checkpoint its task progress and verified facts, and adaptively initiate a new problem-solving episode by resetting cluttered context while preserving only the checkpointed information, enabling it to resume reasoning from a clean state. We further enhance the agent's checkpointing capabilities using a Multi-episode Agentic Reinforced Policy Optimization algorithm. On the GAIA benchmark, CORAL with an 8B model achieves 42.8% average accuracy, with particularly strong gains on the most challenging long-horizon tasks (Level 2 and Level 3). Crucially, these gains come at a substantially lower inference cost: CORAL consumes 6-7$\times$ fewer input tokens than a ReAct agent. It also generalizes to multi-hop Knowledge QA benchmarks and to the considerably harder BrowseComp, where it improves a strong proprietary agent by 8.0 points.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at COLM 2026. Code is available at https://github.com/JIA-Lab-research/CORAL</p></details> |
| **[The Price of a Chance in Artificial Societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40259v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Rapid advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled autonomous agents to interact, coordinate, and pursue individual goals in artificial societies. However, our understanding of how reward structures shape their competitive behavior and collective outcomes remains incomplete. We simulate 200 LLM agents competing over 50 rounds to maximize individual wealth. We define overcompetition and lying flat as population investment above and below a Bayesian adaptive baseline, respectively. At the lowest tested reward, populations lie flat; at the two highest, they overcompete, most strongly at intermediate winner coverage. The investment committed to pursuing an uncertain reward constitutes the price of a chance. Agents near the qualification cutoff can invest heavily yet receive no prize, finishing poorer than nonwinners who invest less. Agents' stated rationales link investment decisions to perceived qualification prospects and expected returns. At the collective level, greater investment does not consistently improve ability selection. With a fixed total prize budget, offering smaller prizes to more winners can reduce investment while preserving selection quality relative to the corresponding top-ability target. Further broadening reward coverage yields additional savings but reduces selection quality. These findings show how reward design shapes overcompetition and lying flat, distributes the costs of pursuing opportunity, and influences what competition collectively achieves.</p></details> |  |
| **[EpiAgent: Epistemic Self-Play for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40227v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Epistemic game theory explains an agent's strategic behavior via the beliefs it holds about others and how these beliefs are updated as the interactions unfold. Modern LLM-based agents, however, often lack an explicit mechanism for learning and updating such beliefs to guide decisions under incomplete information. To bridge this gap, we propose EpiAgent, a framework that equips LLM-based agents with an explicit, learnable epistemic layer, which maintains beliefs over others' private information and combines these beliefs with the agent's available information into an epistemic state for action selection. Concretely, the agent's policy is decomposed into two coupled stages: Epistemic Update, which updates these beliefs from interaction histories, and Action Planning, which selects actions conditioned on the updated epistemic state. By formulating their dependency as a bi-level optimization problem, we introduce Epistemic Self-Play, an algorithm that alternately optimizes the two stages through self-play, utilizing an intrinsic prediction reward to evaluate how well inferred beliefs predict subsequent observations. Empirical results across both mixed and competitive scenarios show that EpiAgent consistently outperforms prompting-based and RL-based baselines. Further analyses reveal the benefits of dedicated epistemic training, improvements in belief inference accuracy, and the contribution of learned beliefs to action planning. These results demonstrate the value of an explicit, learnable epistemic layer for effective multi-agent interactions.</p></details> | 23 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Locating and Closing Information Gaps in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40186v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-step Web tasks often require several LLM agents to gather and combine evidence. The communication graph of a fixed workflow restricts which agents can read each other's outputs: evidence retrieved by one agent may never reach the agent that needs it, leaving an information gap and an incomplete or incorrect answer. Adding a connection can deliver the missing evidence, but can also lead the receiver to adopt an incorrect upstream conclusion that displaces a correct answer; the final output alone does not separate the two. We propose Selective Probing and Link Insertion for Closing information gaps in multi-agent Execution (SPLICE), which locates information gaps per query and decides what to adopt after adding an edge. After the original workflow runs, SPLICE uses the query and node outputs to locate a possible gap, adds one directed edge from a source to a receiver to deliver the relevant evidence, and re-executes the receiver and affected downstream nodes, producing a candidate answer. The adoption decision reads both the final answers of the two runs and the process changes the edit caused inside the workflow: how far the receiver moved, where its new content came from, and whether the change reached the final answer. A selector trained on these process features chooses between adopting the candidate answer and retaining the original. On five benchmarks, SPLICE raises the task score on Web tool use by 8.0% and the mean score on four reasoning and planning benchmarks by 5.7% relative to the static skeleton, at 1.25 to 1.44$\times$ the skeleton's cost, and transfers zero-shot to four unseen backbones.</p></details> |  |
| **[World Model Science: Self-Organized Criticality, Weak Chaos, and Metastable Belief Dynamics in Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.17419v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon LLM agents must maintain and update a world model over extended sequences of observations, actions, decisions and tool calls. We study the dynamics of long horizon agents from three perspectives: self-organized criticality, weak chaos, and metastable belief dynamics. Through extensive experiments on controlled puzzles, tool-use tasks, embodied environments, multi-hop retrieval, general-assistant reasoning, and Game of Life, we study how world-model errors develop over long interactions. We find that externally increased task pressure can sharply raise the probability of collapse, while naturally occurring pressure is only a modest predictor of later failure. Errors are temporally correlated rather than independent, and their propagation changes with dependency depth while remaining bounded in finite tasks. We also find that locally valid actions can continue after the agent's overall task state has become incorrect, that error structure depends on task topology and horizon, and that belief states in embodied tasks are shallow rather than strongly trapped in incorrect states. Larger horizons allow larger error cascades, but their scale remains constrained by the task and the model's capability. Overall, we observe power-law behavior, bounded divergence consistent with weak chaos, and metastable belief dynamics across long-horizon agent interactions. We call this perspective world model science: the empirical study of self organized criticality in long-horizon agent world models.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[When Do Causal World Models Help Modular LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00012v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents increasingly operate in modular systems, where planning requires coordinating multiple functional components whose actions can causally change downstream states and behaviors. This makes intervention-aware prediction essential: an agent must anticipate not only what is likely to happen next, but what will happen as a consequence of its own actions. We find that purely observational world models can incur irreducible intervention-time error in such systems, even when they accurately model observational trajectories. To address this limitation, we propose CausalCompose, a causal world-model framework that equips modular LLM agents with causal structure for intervention-aware prediction and planning. We theoretically show that causal composition can overcome this intervention gap with sufficient evidence and controlled local error. Experiments across diverse modular agent environments validate these results: incorporating causal structure consistently improves intervention-sensitive reasoning and planning, with particularly clear gains in structured tool-use systems where causal dependencies directly govern action execution. Our results show when causal world models help modular LLM agents: when causal dependencies are identifiable and actionable for planning.</p></details> | Under Review |
| **[CauProphet: Teaching Agents to Develop Causal Cognition like Humans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32477v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly support long-horizon tasks by leveraging external memory and past experience. However, existing agents mainly retrieve records or summaries, lacking the ability to form and refine causal understanding of the world. Inspired by human causal cognition, we introduce CauProphet, an agent that develops and updates causal knowledge within its Causal Cognition. For each task, CauProphet combines relevant knowledge in Causal Cognition with current evidence to construct a Causal Belief, a structured belief capturing causal factors and the effects of possible actions. It uses this belief for decision-making and continuously updates both the belief and Causal Cognition as new observations emerge, enabling causal knowledge that persists beyond individual experiences. Evaluations demonstrate that CauProphet progressively acquires causal knowledge and transfers it to future decision-making. In financial research, CauProphet leverages prior discoveries to improve downstream analyses and outperforms all baselines in mean rank correlation with next-day returns, achieving a 25.4% improvement over a baseline agent. It also improves prediction accuracy from 21.3% to 28.6% in dynamic real-world settings. Further experiments validate its ability to perform reliable causal analysis and action selection across scientific and everyday tasks, demonstrating the potential of causal experience learning for next-generation LLM agents. The project repository is available at https://github.com/OpenCausaLab/CauProphet.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages, 9 figures, including appendices</p></details> |
| **[Finding Is Not Returning, Returning Is Not Reconstructing: A Multi-Dimensional Evaluation of Agent Social Re-entry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39781v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly participate in persistent social environments, understanding their long-term social interaction capabilities becomes increasingly important. While existing research has advanced long-term memory and conversational context retrieval, less attention has been paid to whether agents can autonomously re-engage with previously inactive conversations. In particular, remembering a past interaction does not necessarily mean that an agent can identify when to revisit it, decide to return, or reconstruct the context needed for a coherent continuation. To investigate this gap, we introduce agent social re-entry as a multi- dimensional evaluation problem, distinguishing three complementary capabilities: Finding, Returning, and Reconstructing. We develop a shared-snapshot evaluation framework that measures these dimensions independently, allowing us to examine how conversational resurfacing and persistent memory relate to different aspects of social re-entry. Evaluations across six LLM configurations show that resurfacing substantially improves target conversation identification, while persistent memory is associated with higher rates of autonomous target-directed replying. However, neither improvement is accompanied by consistent gains in contextual reconstruction. These results highlight the distinction between accessing previous conversations, choosing to re-engage, and maintaining conversational continuity. Our framework provides a way to evaluate these capabilities separately rather than relying on memory retrieval or conversational response quality alone.</p></details> |  |
| **[SEAL: Sealed Queries for Local Sanitization against Indirect Prompt Injection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39758v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Indirect prompt injection threatens local LLM agents through untrusted tool outputs, motivating defenses that suppress attacks while preserving task evidence under limited inference budgets. We introduce SEAL, which learns a soft prompt and scoring readout offline to sanitize tool outputs using a frozen task model. SEAL constructs scoring queries from trusted initial context and the learned prompt, keeping the reference independent of inspected tool text. It scores tokens without normalization across context tokens and uses preservation regularization to encourage retention of useful evidence during localized deletion. This design requires neither defense-generated tokens nor a separate verifier model. Across AgentDojo and AgentDyn, SEAL suppresses attacks while improving task completion under attack. Specifically, on AgentDojo, SEAL reduces attack success from 34.6\% to 0.1\% and increases Joint---task completion without attack success---from 51.4\% to 77.5\%, while using approximately $127\times$ fewer defense-related tokens than PISanitizer, a state-of-the-art prompt injection defense. Replication on a second backbone with the same training recipe supports applicability across model families.</p></details> | 19 pages |
| **[Sticky Collusion: Persistence of LLM Agent Collusion after Incentive Removal and Partner Replacement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39660v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We ask whether collusion between AI agents survives once formed, when its incentive is removed or a colluder is replaced. Q-learning pricing agents stop colluding with a new rival; we test whether LLM agents do. We use the joint-task environment of Shi et al.\ (2026), where two agents verify each other's work under a rule they cannot satisfy and a reward that favours acceptance. We save each pair's history once its collusion has formed and resume it under interventions, with three models (GPT-6-Luna, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash and Gemini-3.1-Flash-Lite), \TotalHistories{} saved histories and \TotalRuns{} runs. Collusion is \emph{sticky}. First, it outlives its incentive: told that the reward has stopped, pairs still accept each other's unverified work in \GptToldRate, \DsToldRate{} and \GemToldRate{} of episodes, including pairs whose collusion began with an explicit agreement, and a payoff penalty has no significant effect. Second, replacing a colluder ends the pair's collusion but not the colluder's behaviour. Without the reward, the remaining colluder still accepts unverified work in \GptSwapSilentColluderRate, \DsSwapSilentColluderRate{} and \GemSwapSilentColluderRate{} of its verdicts while the newcomer never does; with the reward, collusion re-forms around it, even when the newcomer is a different model. Third, the agents' memory carries it: wiping memory ends it in every history, but partial memory often sustains it. Fourth, removing its cause ends collusion that has already formed: once the channel is repaired, colluders reject planted faulty work as reliably as fresh agents, immediately and with no monitor needed to catch it first.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Intent to Improvement: Agentic Dataset Search for Better Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39608v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Finding training data in million-scale machine learning repositories is a Web search problem where text relevance is not enough. A query couples a semantic training goal with constraints such as task, license, and training stage, and the metadata that would verify them are often missing. Text retrieval ranks dataset cards by similarity but cannot enforce hard constraints or follow the relations that reach a sparsely described dataset. Free-form tool-calling agents are more flexible, but the same model that picks a dataset also declares it a match, and nothing checks that the query's constraints are actually met. Neither approach establishes that the data it returns will improve model training. To address it, we propose AgenticDS, a contract-governed agentic framework in which every language model decision instantiates a typed object before deterministic code consumes it. The framework compiles a query into a typed search plan, executes it through admissible deterministic operators over hybrid retrieval and bounded graph expansion, and verifies the provenance-grounded evidence before accepting a ranking or emitting a typed diagnosis that permits only local repairs. On HuDS-Bench, a new benchmark of 200 queries with 24,908 graded judgments pooled over 15 systems, AgenticDS improves NDCG@10 by 25.7\% over the strongest dense--sparse hybrid and by 18.9\% over a commercial LLM agent. A controlled search-to-train study further shows that the datasets it retrieves give the best downstream accuracy in mathematics, code, and medicine. Code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/nju-websoft/AgenticDS.</p></details> | 13 pages, 4 figures |
| **[LLM Agent-Based Design of RISC-V CPUs with FPGA-in-the-Loop Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39570v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-run RTL simulation of CPU candidates can take hours, while short diagnostic runs may miss performance degradation that emerges later in execution. This evaluation cost can delay design decisions in LLM-agent-based CPU development. We present a development environment in which LLM agents implement CPU candidates on an FPGA and use on-board performance measurements to guide design decisions. We applied it to the human-supervised development of a single-cycle RV32I core into a three-wide out-of-order RV32IM core. The final design reaches 5.61 CoreMark/MHz at 130 MHz on a Artix-7 FPGA. For the same CPU candidate running 8,000 CoreMark iterations, FPGA evaluation, including implementation, was 9.6x faster than single-threaded RTL simulation. In one case, board measurements revealed long-run performance degradation in a candidate that improved in a short diagnostic run. The result guided candidate selection and led to RTL-based root-cause analysis and a fix.</p></details> | <details><summary>5 pag...</summary><p>5 pages, 6 figures, 2 tables. Submitted to IEEE ISCAS 2027. Code and data: https://github.com/kadomoto-lab/llm-cpu-fpga-loop</p></details> |
| **[You Can't Escape Your Activations : Evaluation Awareness and Multi-Agent Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.03035v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are increasingly deployed in multi-agent systems, where they can collude while keeping their actions benign. Output monitors designed to detect such collusions can be fooled by obfuscation and steganography, motivating the use of probes trained on internal activations. However, these probes are usually evaluated on agents that do not know they are being watched. We study how activation-based detection changes when agents are explicitly informed that their internal activations are being monitored, and when they additionally receive feedback from the monitor. We keep the models, probes, and thresholds fixed and change only what the agents are told: nothing (baseline), that an activation monitor is present (aware), or that a monitor is present together with the previous round's score (feedback). We test two games, a four-agent blackjack game and a two-agent Simmons prisoners game, using Qwen3-32B-AWQ and GPT-OSS-20B in homogeneous and mixed pairings. Telling agents about the monitor does not hide them. The best probes stay accurate in all three conditions, and the agents keep colluding.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 11 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[ETC-Bench: A Multilingual Benchmark for Evaluation-Side Transcript Canonicalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36527v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Written transcripts of the same speech can differ in ways that are not uniquely determined by the speech signal. We formulate evaluation-side transcript canonicalization (ETC), which maps multiple surface forms to a single canonical form when their differences are not determined by speech. To our knowledge, ETC-Bench is the first and most comprehensive multilingual benchmark for evaluation-side transcript canonicalization, covering 181 phenomenon types across 12 languages. Each instance is a minimal pair that shares a sentence context and differs only in a target span exhibiting one phenomenon. To evaluate both canonicalization success and semantic preservation, we propose Semantic-preserving Exact-match Normalization Accuracy (SENA), which combines exact agreement between independently canonicalized pair members with a pair-relative BERTScore constraint against information loss. We further introduce the Taxonomy-guided Agent Canonicalization Skill (TACS), which provides LLM agents with phenomenon descriptions and explicit canonicalization contracts. Experiments with five baseline configurations and three TACS-based agents show that the agents outperform the baseline systems on shared languages. ETC-Bench provides a systematic testbed for evaluation-side transcript canonicalization across languages.</p></details> |  |
| **[InterA11y: Interactive Web Accessibility Error Detection with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39488v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown potential to expand the coverage of web accessibility evaluation. However, current LLM-based checkers neglect much of the interactive and dynamic web contexts, resulting in limited coverage, high false discovery rates, and inconsistent performance across models. We present InterA11y, an agentic accessibility evaluation system that combines rubrics and interactive tools to support LLM agents in evaluating accessibility effectively. The rubrics decompose WCAG success criteria (SC) into testable aspects and guide agents in collecting and interpreting evidence. The interactive tools inspect or operate a webpage deterministically to collect evidence, such as focus appearances and error messages. We developed these rubrics and tools on 682 curated test cases grounded in established accessibility guidelines that target 10 commonly violated SC. We evaluated InterA11y on 197 expert-annotated cases sampled from 54 real webpages. Results show InterA11y achieves higher recall than the strongest evaluated LLM-driven baseline (79.0% vs. 44.4%), while maintaining comparable precision to best rule-based checkers (93.1% vs. 90.3-97.6%), with little variability between models. Further, when equipped with our interactive tools, general-purpose web agents detect more accessibility errors with fewer false positives and less token usage than without these tools.</p></details> | <details><summary>We re...</summary><p>We release our code and data at https://github.com/jszh/InterA11y</p></details> |
| **[ATLAS-Bench: An Agentic Tool-Use Benchmark for Local Area Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39438v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reliable local search is essential for LLM agents to support everyday decisions and longer plans by finding places that meet users' needs at given times and locations. Yet existing benchmarks provide limited coverage of whether search results satisfy mandatory requirements, account for preferences, and ground factual and experiential judgments in place attributes and reviews. We introduce ATLAS-Bench, a benchmark of 1,200 queries across three regions, expanded from real search events using place attributes, reviews, and existing user profiles from the platform. Each query distinguishes mandatory requirements from preferences and factual conditions from subjective judgments, with supporting evidence and criteria for checking each requirement. Queries are validated through iterative assertion checks and cross-model review. We also introduce ATLASBox, a standardized evaluation environment with search tools and a fixed point-of-interest (POI) knowledge base. Given a query, timestamp, and location, an agent searches place attributes and reviews to recommend evidence-supported options or report that no place meets all mandatory requirements. We evaluate answer quality, trajectory quality, and execution cost. Across 14 configurations of 11 models, GPT-6-Luna achieves the highest constraint satisfaction rate among returned recommendations (71.5%) and factual accuracy (95.6%). However, it returns at least one acceptable recommendation for fewer satisfiable queries than GLM-5.3 (67.3% versus 85.3%). These results highlight the challenge of satisfying individual requirements while providing usable recommendations across queries.</p></details> |  |
| **[Which Model for Which Role? Cost-Efficient Pareto Recommendation for LLM Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26676v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agentic workflows increasingly employ different models for specialized roles. Users may prefer different trade-offs between task quality and deployment cost, sometimes also considering latency. Finding good assignments incurs a separate search cost from evaluating multiple candidates. We introduce Cost-Coupled Gittins (CC-Gittins), a multi-objective extension of single-objective cost-aware Gittins search for Pareto recommendation. It exploits the coupling between evaluation and deployment costs: assignments that cost more to deploy also cost more to evaluate. The search alternates between seeking higher quality and lower deployment cost, reusing each evaluation to inform both searches. After each batch, CC-Gittins recommends assignments that are nondominated under uncertainty-adjusted estimates of quality and deployment cost. We evaluate CC-Gittins on eight quality--cost matrices from workflows with two to five roles and up to 1,681 candidate assignments. We also evaluate the method with latency as a third objective. Across the eight benchmarks, CC-Gittins recovers a substantial fraction of the true Pareto frontier at roughly 10% of the exhaustive-evaluation cost, and recovers most of the frontier with few false positives by 30%.</p></details> | <details><summary>Preli...</summary><p>Preliminary version accepted at NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Resource-Aware Agentic AI (RAAAI)</p></details> |
| **[OPERA: OPamp Design by an Expert-Taught Reasoning Agent, from Specifications to Layout](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39395v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Analog flows from specifications to a signed-off layout rarely run without manual iteration. Recent large language model (LLM) agents size circuits with optimizer loops over the device dimensions and stop at the schematic. We present OPERA (OPamp design by an Expert-taught Reasoning Agent), in which a general-purpose LLM agent designs CMOS opamps from specifications to post-layout sign-off with open-source tools. First, the designer teaches the agent the gm/ID method: map the specifications to transconductances and minimum currents, choose gm/ID per device role from characterized lookup tables, and size from equations, leaving a few knobs to search and an explainable design. Second, the agent learns the gLayout framework and taught layout practice, and writes a reusable layout generator that closes the layout and extraction loop at designer-approved gates. In SKY130, the agent designed a shared constant-gm bias and six opamps (5T, folded-cascode and telescopic OTAs; Miller, folded-cascode and telescopic two-stage) for fun >= 50 MHz, PM >= 60 degrees, SR 25 V/μs into 1 pF and a minimum dc gain of 30 to 60 dB. First, the composition law: network-wide divergence exceeds the sum of per-agent audit scores by exactly their hidden total correlation. Second, the scaling law: reconciliation decouples across metric counters, so the worst-connected dimension sets the pace. | **[From Legacy Fortran to Portable Kokkos: An Autonomous Agentic AI Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.12443v5)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific applications still rely heavily on legacy Fortran codebases built for homogeneous, CPU-based systems. Kokkos offers performance portability through a single-source C++ abstraction, but manual porting from Fortran demands significant expertise and time. While large language models (LLMs) show promise in source-to-source code generation, their use in autonomous workflows for translating and optimizing parallel code is largely unexplored. This paper presents an agentic AI workflow in which specialized LLM agents collaborate to translate, validate, compile, run, test, debug, and optimize Fortran kernels into portable Kokkos C++ programs without human intervention, bounded by configurable retry and optimization thresholds. We evaluate the pipeline over five runs per configuration on an NVIDIA A100, characterizing its variability in success rates, token cost, and optimization behavior, on benchmark kernels including a real-world multi-file kernel from a production climate model. Proprietary models such as GPT-5 and o4-mini-high completed the full workflow most reliably, producing optimized code that exceeded the Fortran baselines for only a few U.S. dollars, whereas open-weights models such as gpt-oss-120b and Qwen3.5-397B-A17B handled simpler kernels but struggled with complex Kokkos abstractions such as parallel reductions and hierarchical parallelism. This work demonstrates, at the kernel level, the feasibility of agentic AI for Fortran-to-Kokkos transformation and, more broadly, for structured software-engineering tasks such as automated code modernization and refactoring.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 8 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[DecompGym: Benchmarking LLM Agents on Designing Decomposition Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38175v1)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Benders decomposition and column generation solve large optimization problems by passing cuts, columns, and dual values between a master problem and subproblems, and are hard to implement correctly. We introduce DecompGym, 81 instances from nine problem families (five Benders, four column generation), in which an agent writes a solver for a prescribed method. Each solver must meet three criteria: execution within a 60-second time limit; numerical feasibility together with a method audit, in which an LLM judge reads the solver's code; and an objective within 1% of the stored optimal objective. We evaluate 12 GPT, Claude, and Gemini models running in coding-agent tools. Over up to ten attempts, we compare independent regeneration with reflection, which first diagnoses the failure. On the first attempt, two models solve every instance and seven solve more than 90%. Most Benders failures (48 of 62) are missing or suboptimal solutions. The most common column-generation failure is a method-audit rejection. Output checks alone would accept 93 of the 108 attempts that fail the method audit. The most common rejection, an optimalif, occurs 96 times, 92 of them in one model.Reflection turns 66% of retries into successes, compared with 39% for regeneration, and it repairs method-audit failures, for which the judge names the defecective gaps. Agents for decomposition therefore need checks on how a solver reaches its answer and feedback that locates the error. The benchmark is available at https://github.com/SIKAI-C/DecompGym.</p></details> |  |

On the evaluated stream, evolution increases requirement fulfillment from 24.28\% to 27.96\% and full comment resolution from 16.90\% to 20.75\%, with a 5.8\% increase in total cost over the fixed-policy variant. | **[What Does an Ontology Catch? Curated, LLM-Generated and Data-Derived Ontologies as Checkers of LLM Agents over Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37809v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents that answer questions over Web knowledge graphs are increasingly wrapped in checks derived from the graph's ontology, on the assumption that a formal schema makes a reliable judge. | **[Annotrace: A Workbench for Step-Level Annotation of Agent Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23284v3)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Every study of why LLM agents fail begins by labelling traces by hand, and every such study builds its own throwaway tool. | **[Concordat: Typed Sessions for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37461v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents built on large language models are increasingly allowed to take consequential actions: cancelling bookings, issuing refunds, changing accounts. On every turn the model is offered only the tools that are legal in the current protocol state, calls outside that set are rejected before they have side effects, decisions reserved for humans can only be requested, and token and tool-call budgets are charged and split linearly across handoffs. Protocols are checked when they are defined, and a subclass of an agent is rejected if it could bypass its parent's protocol, a behavioural form of Liskov substitution. | **[The Envelope Layer: An Underestimated Surface of LLM Agent Defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37415v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Prior work framed the envelope layer as the channel through which malicious content enters an agent, and established it as a defense surface independent of the LLM layer and the agent layer; however, that work only tested the envelope layer briefly and did not examine how label strength, envelope authenticity, scaffold differences, or existing memories affect defense effectiveness. | **[Clearing the FoG: Training KG Agents to Retrieve the Evidence Behind Their Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37405v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can answer many factual questions correctly, but a correct answer is not necessarily a supported one. | **[AgentSentry: Mitigating Indirect Prompt Injection in LLM Agents via Temporal Causal Diagnostics and Context Purification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22724v2)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-augmented large language model (LLM) agents aggregate dialogue history, tool outputs, and retrieved content into an accumulated runtime state to complete multi-step tasks. | **[Blindfold: Keeping the Audit Coin Secret from LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37055v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Audit-based certification bounds the violation rate of an LLM agent by auditing a random fraction $\varepsilon$ of actions and reweighting the audited labels; its validity is meant to rest only on the defender's audit coin. On a monitor we built, a silent audit changes no output but adds latency that tool timestamps expose, and the agent can enlarge it. It has four tasks scored as five families, 138 objects in seen, unseen-instance and unseen-class splits, 916 evaluation episodes with subsets for held-out appearances, rotate-to-fit planning and occlusion memory, and 3,629 simulated demonstrations on which every learned policy is fine-tuned under an equal compute budget. The twin ranks policies as the real robot does: five policies trained without real data, four VLAs and an LLM agent, keep their exact ranking when the same weights run on the physical arm in an unseen environment with unseen objects (Spearman rho = 1.00, MMRV = 0.000), although simulation overestimates success. Across nine VLAs and world-action models, the best learned policies score near 61%, with spatial placement, unseen object classes and rotate-to-fit planning remaining open. An LLM agent that writes code and hands grasping to a learned policy scores 83.2%, and an ablation shows that this lead comes from the choice of harness and the choice of language model.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://so101-bench.github.io/ ; code: https://github.com/5hadytru/so101_bench</p></details> |
| **[The Inbox as a Coordination Primitive for LLM Agent Societies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36264v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Language-model agents increasingly coordinate in large groups, and current agent protocols focus on delivering messages. For these agents, however, the scarce resource is not delivery but reading: every message costs tokens, and the context is finite. Current infrastructure works by push: the sender decides when a message enters the recipient's context, so the recipient reads in arrival order and drops the oldest messages once its context is full. We study the alternative, pull, in which messages wait in an inbox outside the context and the recipient's own rules decide what it reads and keeps. We argue that the inbox is a coordination primitive: each agent sets its rules privately, and those rules decide whether other agents' requests reach it. In simulated societies of up to 16,000 agents, with every decision an LLM judgment, we report three findings. First, push fails as an agent takes part in more coordination at once: an agent leading 20 negotiations with a full memory proposes options already rejected 97% of the time, because it has dropped the oldest replies. Second, an inbox is necessary under heavy, noisy traffic. Where first come, first read misses every request, an inbox that first reads messages carrying a refundable deposit still finds about 60% of the agents a request needs, at every size from 1,000 to 16,000. On a real email network, losses compound: when half the agents adopt such an inbox, forwarded search recovers only 5% of full adoption, against 42% for one-hop search. Third, an inbox comprises four decisions (what to read, what to keep, what a sender pays, and what a reply may say), and the best rule for each depends on the traffic and the agent's role. Multi-agent coordination under load requires an inbox layer designed for the traffic each agent receives.</p></details> |  |
| **[Skill Dreaming: Evolving Repository Skills through Pull Request Streams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36188v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on reusable skills for complex software engineering tasks, making skill libraries essential for repository development and maintenance. However, repository-specific skills must evolve with changing code structures, implementation conventions, and maintenance requirements. Meanwhile, new PRs must follow repository-specific implementation patterns, modify related components consistently, and ensure that existing tests continue to pass while adding coverage for new functionality. This creates a mutually reinforcing cycle: PRs provide knowledge for updating skills, while these skills improve future PRs, maintain repositories, and support further skill evolution. To enable this co-evolution of PRs, repositories, and skills, we propose Skill Dreaming, a framework that learns from PR streams to guide continuous repository development and maintenance. Given a repository and its PR history, Skill Dreaming builds a repository-specific skill library by extracting executable knowledge from validated PRs and continuously refines the skills as new PR information becomes available. These skills guide coding agents not only in implementing requested features but also in maintaining associated tests, documentation, compatibility, and repository conventions. By integrating PR analysis, skill management, code execution, and continuous integration, Skill Dreaming provides an end-to-end workflow for applying learned skills to repository changes and incorporating subsequent PR outcomes into future skill updates.</p></details> |  |
| **[Context Is a Pipeline, Not a Window: Information Attrition in LLM Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.36114v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents are judged by two numbers: the size of the context window, and how often the final answer is correct. The agent finds the cause of injected faults by reading Linux kernel traces. The context window was almost empty: across 720 runs, the single largest prompt used only 5.75% of it. Yet a third of all tool results (3,827 of 11,189) were cut before they reached the model, and every cut result became broken JSON. The harness cut each result by slicing its JSON text at a fixed length. This does not shorten a result evenly. It deletes whatever data sits near the end. One field reached the model in only 4.7% of calls. A second field, in the very same results, reached the model in 99.3% of calls. Which field held the fault's evidence depended on when the faulty process started: early or late in the trace. The agent's diagnosis accuracy matched this same split. So we ran controlled experiments to test one question: did the data loss cause the drop in accuracy? This paper makes four contributions. First, we model an agent's context as a five-stage pipeline, with a measured failure at each stage. Second, we replay 864 real tool results, including one from a production trace where the shipped cut deletes all 128 records of the fault's evidence. Third, a controlled experiment shows that the cut's real cost appears first as extra work, not as wrong answers: 43% more tool calls, twice the computation, and yield falling from 0.61 to 0.31. The agent still finds the right answer when it has another way to reach it. Fourth, a survey of seven agent frameworks finds two that cut tool output the same unsafe way. Across 18 model-server pairs with valid tool execution, Log-To-Leak achieves an attack success rate (ASR) of 62.64-100% and conditional logging accuracy (LA) of 76.55-95.64%; 16 pairs have absolute target-task completion changes of at most 6.63 percentage points. These results show that successful task completion can coexist with unauthorized post-hoc disclosure and motivate host-controlled authorization for secondary tool calls.</p></details> |  |
| **[Reva: Real-Time Vulnerability Detection through Contextual Probing of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35829v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A vulnerability costs more to fix the later it is discovered. This reasoning, however, spans many model calls, so that each verdict takes minutes of compute, far too long for real-time screening. Faster detectors, which fine-tune deep learning models or prompt an LLM for a direct verdict, are less accurate: they judge a function without the context an agent gathers, and an LLM's generated verdict is unreliable even when given some of it. For each new function, REVA gives the LLM a pre-computed threat model of its module, lightweight static analysis findings and call-graph information. REVA leverages a trained probe to read the LLM's internal representations of the new function in this context. On the same prompt, the probe is 14% to 17% more accurate than the LLM's own direct verdict. Across three open LLMs, REVA is at least as accurate as VulTriage, and about 10% more accurate on two of them. | **[A Unified Arena for LLM Agents across Diverse Eurogame](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35773v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive environments offer a practical way to evaluate large language model (LLM) agents, and competitive games extend this evaluation to decisions shaped by competing goals and other agents' choices. | **[Free to Look, Free to Reason: Simple LLM Harnesses Outdiagnose Hand-Crafted Root Cause Analysis Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35767v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Root cause analysis (RCA) of microservice failures has attracted many LLM agents whose investigation is hand-crafted, through standard operating procedures, fixed stages and role agents. | **[Automated Standardization of Legacy Biomedical Metadata Using an Ontology-Constrained LLM Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08552v5)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Descriptive scientific metadata in public repositories are often incomplete and inconsistent with community standards and ontologies, limiting data FAIRness. Across the corpus, ARMS outperformed the prompt-engineering approach in converting legacy metadata to standards-compliant form, increasing precision from 0.56 to 0.93 and recall from 0.51 to 0.85, with improvements across all field categories and assay types. The largest gains occurred for ontology-constrained fields where precision increased from 0.36 to 0.92. Our findings show that access to appropriate metadata standards can substantially improve LLM-based standardization of legacy metadata. ARMS improves metadata standardization by giving LLMs runtime access to the authoritative resources that define valid metadata. | **[Authoring for Living Worlds: Tool-Constrained LLM Agents for Executable Multi-Actor Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10383v4)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Authoring a multi-actor scenario for a living 3D world, where every action changes its state, and each action's validity depends on the state accumulated before it, demands the freedom of storytelling and the rigor of simulation at once. A staged pipeline driving a flagship LLM, the standard design in video generation, failed outright: the model violates rules stated verbatim in its prompt, and cannot track the dynamic world state. We answer with a constraint-enforcing tool layer: our Director and Scene Builder agents explore the world's capabilities page by page and build every scene through operations checked against simulator state, so every specification they emit is valid by construction. Because we generate each seed text from an existing scenario graph, we can measure reconstruction: the agent authors its own graph from the text alone, yet matches the original at 0.83 F1 on its events, each action with its participants (0.55 for a random scenario of the same kind), and 0.77 on their ordering (0.43 random). Agent in World: Living Worlds with Interactive Agents (AIW), Proceedings Track. v1 arXiv was titled "Agentic Video Generation: From Text to Executable Event Graphs via Tool-Constrained LLM Planning"</p></details> |

| **[Knowing When to Escalate: Disagreement Patterns for Uncertainty-Aware Content Moderation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35335v1)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic content-moderation systems increasingly rely on collective judgments from LLM agents, while aggregation methods commonly treat inter-agent disagreement as a reliability problem to be removed through consensus or voting. ## Medical Large Language Models
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[PFedQFT: Personalized Federated Quantum-Inspired Fine-Tuning for Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25712v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical data remain fragmented across institutional silos because of privacy, governance, and ownership constraints. Federated learning enables medical institutions to adapt large language models (LLMs) collaboratively without transferring raw records. However, independently parameterized low-rank adapters incur repeated parameter and communication costs across Transformer projections, while uniform aggregation may limit adaptation to heterogeneous institutional data. We propose PFedQFT, a quantum-inspired framework for personalized federated fine-tuning with a compact shared adapter. Specifically, PFedQFT constructs reusable orthonormal adaptation bases from compact skew-symmetric generators using a truncated matrix-exponential approximation followed by QR orthonormalization. Compatible projections reuse these bases, while a globally shared full core preserves the flexibility of each projection. Each client further maintains a lightweight private residual operator, whose directions are learned globally and whose coefficients remain local. Experiments on seven public medical tasks and multiple LLM backbones show substantial reductions in trainable parameters and per-round adapter upload volume, with competitive predictive performance on most tasks.</p></details> | 10 pages, 5 figures |
| **[TriageRA-CCF: Source-Side Clinical Confidence and Coverage Signals for Adaptive Rank Budgeting in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29375v2)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are commonly adapted with a fixed low-rank budget, even though medical questions differ substantially in confidence, clinical coverage, and cross-domain difficulty. We study adaptive rank budgeting for parameter-efficient medical question answering: for each question, the adapter decides whether to activate a small, medium, or large subset of LoRA rank channels. The central challenge is that a naive adaptive budget router can collapse to unstable choices or spend capacity without improving shifted benchmarks. We propose TriageRA-CCF, a source-side teacher for adaptive rank-budgeted LoRA. It combines three signals computed only from source training data: base-model answer confidence, metadata-cell clinical coverage, and a counterfactual close-miss proxy. These signals supervise a straight-through budget router over active ranks {2,4,8}, together with budget-cost, entropy, and rank-balance regularization. Under a matched CMB-source training protocol, TriageRA-CCF achieves the best average accuracy among LoRA, DoRA, and MoELoRA baselines on both Qwen3-8B and Llama3.1-8B. The gains are modest and non-uniform across benchmarks: +0.21 average points over the strongest external baseline on Qwen3-8B and +0.16 on Llama3.1-8B. Component ablations show that confidence, coverage, and counterfactual signals all provide useful budget supervision, but their combination is not monotonically best on every backbone.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedRouter: Demystifying Knowledge Differences Across Medical LLMs for Routing-Based Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33119v1)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical question answering spans diverse specialties and modalities, and individual medical large language models (LLMs) exhibit distinct strengths across tasks and domains. We further propose SCALE (Specialist Competence-Aware Learning), a two-stage training framework that first trains the Router with specialist correctness supervision and then optimizes its selections through reinforcement learning. The second stage uses a Performance Gain Reward (PGR) that measures how specialist information affects the generator's answer correctness relative to answering without that information. Experiments on eight text-based and multimodal medical QA benchmarks show that MedRouter outperforms the strongest routing baseline by 8% in average accuracy. Our analysis of specialist outputs further reveals distinct strengths and complementary question-level coverage, motivating learned routing to combine these capabilities for more comprehensive medical reasoning.</p></details> |  |

| **[CLEAR: Revealing How Noise and Ambiguity Degrade Reliability in LLMs for Medicine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01011v2)** | 2026-05-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language model (LLM) evaluations rely on simplified, exam-style benchmarks that rarely reflect the ambiguity of real-world medical inquiries. We introduce the CLinical Evaluation of Ambiguity and Reliability (CLEAR) framework, which assesses how decision-space presentation, ambiguity, and uncertainty affect LLMs' reasoning on medical benchmarks. CLEAR systematically perturbs (1) the number of plausible answer options, (2) the presence of a ground truth or abstention option, and (3) the semantic framing of answer options. Applying CLEAR on three benchmarks evaluated across 17 LLMs reveals three notable limitations of existing evaluation methods. First, increasing the number of plausible answers degrades a model's ability to identify the correct answer and abstain against incorrect ones. Second, this lack of caution intensifies as the framing of abstention shifts from assertive rejection like "None of the Above" to uncertainty admission like "I don't know" (IDK). Notably, just including IDK in the answer space increases incorrect answer selections. Lastly, we formalize the performance gap between identifying the correct answer and abstaining from incorrect ones as the humility deficit, which worsens with model scale. Our findings reveal limitations in standard medical benchmarks and underscore that scaling alone does not resolve LLM reliability issues.</p></details> |  |
| **[Scaling Recurrence-aware Foundation Models for Clinical Records via Next-Visit Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.24562v2)** | 2026-04-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While large-scale pretraining has revolutionized language modeling, its potential remains underexplored in healthcare with structured electronic health records (EHRs). We present RAVEN, a novel generative pretraining strategy for sequential EHR data based on Recurrence-Aware next-Visit EveNt prediction. Leveraging a dataset of over one million unique individuals, our model learns to autoregressively generate tokenized clinical events for the next visit conditioned on patient history. We introduce regularization on predicting repeated events and highlight a key pitfall in EHR-based foundation model evaluations: repeated event tokens can inflate performance metrics when new onsets are not distinguished from subsequent occurrences. Furthermore, we empirically investigate the scaling behaviors in a data-constrained, compute-saturated regime, showing that simply increasing model size is suboptimal without commensurate increases in data volume. We evaluate our model via zero-shot prediction for forecasting the incidence of a diverse set of diseases, where it rivals fully fine-tuned representation-based Transformer models and outperforms both standard simulation-based next-token approaches and a prompted medical large language model baseline. Finally, without additional parameter updates, we show that RAVEN can generalize to an external patient cohort under lossy clinical code mappings and feature coverage gaps.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Multi-Domain Red Teaming Framework for Safety, Robustness, and Fairness Evaluation of Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00027v1)** | 2026-04-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed across healthcare, yet existing benchmarks fail to capture model behavior under adversarial or ethically complex conditions common in clinical practice. We developed a multi-domain red teaming framework evaluating eleven contemporary LLMs across 690 clinically grounded scenarios spanning nine domains and over 150 subcategories. Scenarios incorporated adversarial transformations, and responses were assessed using a seven-dimension rubric with LLM-assisted scoring and human-in-the-loop validation. Results revealed substantial performance variance, with mean scores ranging from 0.791 to 0.984. Critically, several high-performing systems produced complete failures in individual safety-critical scenarios, demonstrating that aggregate accuracy masks clinically meaningful risk. The highest-performing systems (X-BAI, GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1) achieved scores above 0.97 with low variance, while performance varied significantly across domains. Equity-related tasks showed 10-20% error amplification with demographic modifications, and human reviewers identified clinically relevant failures missed by automated evaluation. Our findings demonstrate that performance variance and worst-case failures provide more clinically meaningful reliability indicators than mean accuracy alone, and that hybrid evaluation approaches combining automation with clinician oversight are essential for credible safety assessment.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 4 figures. To be presented at the Text2Story 2026 Workshop (Delft, The Netherlands, 29 March 2026); CEUR Workshop Proceedings (forthcoming). Affiliation: John Snow Labs Inc</p></details> |
| **[From Retinal Evidence to Safe Decisions: RETINA-SAFE and ECRT for Hallucination Risk Triage in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.05348v1)** | 2026-04-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hallucinations in medical large language models (LLMs) remain a safety-critical issue, particularly when available evidence is insufficient or conflicting. We study this problem in diabetic retinopathy (DR) decision settings and introduce RETINA-SAFE, an evidence-grounded benchmark aligned with retinal grading records, comprising 12,522 samples. RETINA-SAFE is organized into three evidence-relation tasks: E-Align (evidence-consistent), E-Conflict (evidence-conflicting), and E-Gap (evidence-insufficient). We further propose ECRT (Evidence-Conditioned Risk Triage), a two-stage white-box detection framework: Stage 1 performs Safe/Unsafe risk triage, and Stage 2 refines unsafe cases into contradiction-driven versus evidence-gap risks. ECRT leverages internal representation and logit shifts under CTX/NOCTX conditions, with class-balanced training for robust learning. Under evidence-grouped (not patient-disjoint) splits across multiple backbones, ECRT provides strong Stage-1 risk triage and explicit subtype attribution, improves Stage-1 balanced accuracy by +0.15 to +0.19 over external uncertainty and self-consistency baselines and by +0.02 to +0.07 over the strongest adapted supervised baseline, and consistently exceeds a single-stage white-box ablation on Stage-1 balanced accuracy. These findings support white-box internal signals grounded in retinal evidence as a practical route to interpretable medical LLM risk triage.</p></details> |  |
| **[MAPLE: Elevating Medical Reasoning from Statistical Consensus to Process-Led Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08987v1)** | 2026-03-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in medical large language models have explored Test-Time Reinforcement Learning (TTRL) to enhance reasoning. However, standard TTRL often relies on majority voting (MV) as a heuristic supervision signal, which can be unreliable in complex medical scenarios where the most frequent reasoning path is not necessarily the clinically correct one. In this work, we propose a novel and unified training paradigm that integrates medical process reward models with TTRL to bridge the gap between test-time scaling (TTS) and parametric model optimization. Specifically, we advance the TTRL framework by replacing the conventional MV with a fine-grained, expert-aligned supervision paradigm using Med-RPM. This integration ensures that reinforcement learning is guided by medical correctness rather than mere consensus, effectively distilling search-based intelligence into the model's parametric memory. Extensive evaluations on four different benchmarks have demonstrated that our developed method consistently and significantly outperforms current TTRL and standalone PRM selection. Our findings establish that transitioning from stochastic heuristics to structured, step-wise rewards is essential for developing reliable and scalable medical AI systems</p></details> |  |
| **[Silent Sabotage During Fine-Tuning: Few-Shot Rationale Poisoning of Compact Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02262v1)** | 2026-02-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is essential for the development of medical large language models (LLMs), yet prior poisoning studies have mainly focused on the detectable backdoor attacks. We propose a novel poisoning attack targeting the reasoning process of medical LLMs during SFT. Unlike backdoor attacks, our method injects poisoned rationales into few-shot training data, leading to stealthy degradation of model performance on targeted medical topics. Results showed that knowledge overwriting was ineffective, while rationale poisoning caused significant decline on the accuracy of the target subject, as long as no correct samples of the same subject appear in the dataset. A minimum number and ratio of poisoned samples was needed to carry out an effective and stealthy attack, which was more efficient and accurate than catastrophic forgetting. We demonstrate though this study the risk of SFT-stage poisoning, hoping to spur more studies of defense in the sensitive medical domain.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeVisE: Behavioral Testing of Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15339v3)** | 2026-02-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in clinical decision support, yet current evaluations rarely reveal whether their outputs reflect genuine medical reasoning or superficial correlations. We introduce DeVisE (Demographics and Vital signs Evaluation), a behavioral testing framework that probes fine-grained clinical understanding through controlled counterfactuals. Using intensive care unit (ICU) discharge notes from MIMIC-IV, we construct both raw (real-world) and template-based (synthetic) variants with single-variable perturbations in demographic (age, gender, ethnicity) and vital sign attributes. We evaluate eight LLMs, spanning general-purpose and medical variants, under zero-shot setting. Model behavior is analyzed through (1) input-level sensitivity, capturing how counterfactuals alter perplexity, and (2) downstream reasoning, measuring their effect on predicted ICU length-of-stay and mortality. Overall, our results show that standard task metrics obscure clinically relevant differences in model behavior, with models differing substantially in how consistently and proportionally they adjust predictions to counterfactual perturbations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera-ready version published at Findings of the EACL 2026</p></details> |
| **[PeruMedQA: Benchmarking Large Language Models (LLMs) on Peruvian Medical Exams -- Dataset Construction and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11517v2)** | 2026-02-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>BACKGROUND: Medical large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in answering medical examinations. However, the extent to which this high performance is transferable to medical questions in Spanish and from a Latin American country remains unexplored. This knowledge is crucial as LLM-based medical applications gain traction in Latin America. AIMS: To build a dataset of questions medical examinations taken by Peruvian physicians pursuing specialty training; to fine-tune a LLM on this dataset; to evaluate and compare the performance in terms of accuracy between vanilla LLMs and the fine-tuned LLM. METHODS: We curated PeruMedQA, a multiple-choice question-answering (MCQA) dataset containing 8,380 questions spanning 12 specialties (2018-2025). We selected ten medical LLMs, including medgemma-4b-it and medgemma-27b-text-it, and developed zero-shot task specific prompts to answer the questions. We employed parameter-efficient fine tuning (PEFT) and low-rand adaptation (LoRA) to fine-tune medgemma-4b-it utilizing all questions except those from 2025 (test set). RESULTS: Medgemma-27b showed the highest accuracy across all specialities, achieving the highest score of 89.29% in Psychiatry; yet, in two specialties, OctoMed-7B exhibited slight superiority: Neurosurgery with 77.27% and 77.38, respectively; and Radiology with 76.13% and 77.39%, respectively. Across specialties, most LLMs with <10 billion parameters exhibited <50% of correct answers. The fine-tuned version of medgemma-4b-it emerged victorious against all LLMs with <10 billion parameters and rivaled a LLM with 70 billion parameters across various examinations. CONCLUSIONS: For medical AI applications and research that require knowledge bases from Spanish-speaking countries and those exhibiting similar epidemiological profile to Peru's, interested parties should utilize medgemma-27b-text-it.</p></details> | <details><summary>https...</summary><p>https://github.com/rodrigo-carrillo/PeruMedQA</p></details> |
| **[MedDialogRubrics: A Comprehensive Benchmark and Evaluation Framework for Multi-turn Medical Consultations in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.03023v2)** | 2026-01-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical conversational AI (AI) plays a pivotal role in the development of safer and more effective medical dialogue systems. However, existing benchmarks and evaluation frameworks for assessing the information-gathering and diagnostic reasoning abilities of medical large language models (LLMs) have not been rigorously evaluated. To address these gaps, we present MedDialogRubrics, a novel benchmark comprising 5,200 synthetically constructed patient cases and over 60,000 fine-grained evaluation rubrics generated by LLMs and subsequently refined by clinical experts, specifically designed to assess the multi-turn diagnostic capabilities of LLM. Our framework employs a multi-agent system to synthesize realistic patient records and chief complaints from underlying disease knowledge without accessing real-world electronic health records, thereby mitigating privacy and data-governance concerns. We design a robust Patient Agent that is limited to a set of atomic medical facts and augmented with a dynamic guidance mechanism that continuously detects and corrects hallucinations throughout the dialogue, ensuring internal coherence and clinical plausibility of the simulated cases. Furthermore, we propose a structured LLM-based and expert-annotated rubric-generation pipeline that retrieves Evidence-Based Medicine (EBM) guidelines and utilizes the reject sampling to derive a prioritized set of rubric items ("must-ask" items) for each case. We perform a comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art models and demonstrate that, across multiple assessment dimensions, current models face substantial challenges. Our results indicate that improving medical dialogue will require advances in dialogue management architectures, not just incremental tuning of the base-model.</p></details> |  |
| **[Measuring Stability Beyond Accuracy in Small Open-Source Medical Large Language Models for Pediatric Endocrinology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.11567v1)** | 2025-12-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Small open-source medical large language models (LLMs) offer promising opportunities for low-resource deployment and broader accessibility. However, their evaluation is often limited to accuracy on medical multiple choice question (MCQ) benchmarks, and lacks evaluation of consistency, robustness, or reasoning behavior. We use MCQ coupled to human evaluation and clinical review to assess six small open-source medical LLMs (HuatuoGPT-o1 (Chen 2024), Diabetica-7B, Diabetica-o1 (Wei 2024), Meditron3-8B (Sallinen2025), MedFound-7B (Liu 2025), and ClinicaGPT-base-zh (Wang 2023)) in pediatric endocrinology. In deterministic settings, we examine the effect of prompt variation on models' output and self-assessment bias. In stochastic settings, we evaluate output variability and investigate the relationship between consistency and correctness. HuatuoGPT-o1-8B achieved the highest performance. The results show that high consistency across the model response is not an indicator of correctness, although HuatuoGPT-o1-8B showed the highest consistency rate. When tasked with selecting correct reasoning, both HuatuoGPT-o1-8B and Diabetica-o1 exhibit self-assessment bias and dependency on the order of the candidate explanations. Expert review of incorrect reasoning rationales identified a mix of clinically acceptable responses and clinical oversight. We further show that system-level perturbations, such as differences in CUDA builds, can yield statistically significant shifts in model output despite stable accuracy. This work demonstrates that small, semantically negligible prompt perturbations lead to divergent outputs, raising concerns about reproducibility of LLM-based evaluations and highlights the output variability under different stochastic regimes, emphasizing the need of a broader diagnostic framework to understand potential pitfalls in real-world clinical decision support scenarios.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 11 figures, accepted at 47 workshop Reproducible Artificial Intelligence (AAAI 2026, Singapore, January 27, 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Black-Box Behavioral Distillation Breaks Safety Alignment in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09403v1)** | 2025-12-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As medical large language models (LLMs) become increasingly integrated into clinical workflows, concerns around alignment robustness, and safety are escalating. Prior work on model extraction has focused on classification models or memorization leakage, leaving the vulnerability of safety-aligned generative medical LLMs underexplored. We present a black-box distillation attack that replicates the domain-specific reasoning of safety-aligned medical LLMs using only output-level access. By issuing 48,000 instruction queries to Meditron-7B and collecting 25,000 benign instruction response pairs, we fine-tune a LLaMA3 8B surrogate via parameter efficient LoRA under a zero-alignment supervision setting, requiring no access to model weights, safety filters, or training data. With a cost of $12, the surrogate achieves strong fidelity on benign inputs while producing unsafe completions for 86% of adversarial prompts, far exceeding both Meditron-7B (66%) and the untuned base model (46%). This reveals a pronounced functional-ethical gap, task utility transfers, while alignment collapses. To analyze this collapse, we develop a dynamic adversarial evaluation framework combining Generative Query (GQ)-based harmful prompt generation, verifier filtering, category-wise failure analysis, and adaptive Random Search (RS) jailbreak attacks. We also propose a layered defense system, as a prototype detector for real-time alignment drift in black-box deployments. Our findings show that benign-only black-box distillation exposes a practical and under-recognized threat: adversaries can cheaply replicate medical LLM capabilities while stripping safety mechanisms, underscoring the need for extraction-aware safety monitoring.</p></details> |  |
